⏺ I've read the assignment PDF and inspected the seed data. Here's my
  understanding:

  The task: Build an MVP "follow-up engine" for home-service quotes — decide who
  to contact and when, draft a message, then require human approval before
  "sending." Time-boxed to 3 hours, judgment and safety matter more than polish
  (no UI/auth/deployment/framework requirements).

  Seed data (seed/quotes.json, seed/events.jsonl):
  - 30 quotes: open (27), accepted (2), dismissed (1). Fields: id,
    customer_name, customer_phone, tech_name, amount, status, created_at,
    last_contact_at.
  - 88 event lines, 82 unique event_ids — 6 are exact full-record duplicates
    (easy dedupe key). Types present: quote_viewed, customer_replied,
    message_sent, quote_accepted, and quote_sent (not mentioned in the PDF's
    list, but present in data — likely "quote delivered to customer," distinct
    from created_at).
  - Timestamps are genuinely out of file-order (not chronological in the file).
  - Referential integrity is clean: every event's quote_id exists in quotes.json
    and every quote has ≥1 event.
  - One deliberate wrinkle: Q-1003 and Q-1025 are the same customer (Karen
    Nguyen, same phone) with two separate open quotes — relevant to how
    "cooldown per customer" vs "per quote" guardrail should be interpreted.

  What I need to build:
  1. Event ingestion (dedupe, handle out-of-order).
  2. A follow-up policy producing a prioritized, reasoned candidate list for a
     given "now" (e.g. viewed-but-no-response, big quote stale N days, plus
     whatever other signals I justify).
  3. Message drafting.
  4. Draft → approve → send flow with guardrails: cooldown between messages to
     same customer, never message accepted/dismissed quotes, idempotent
     re-run/retry (no double-send).
  5. Some way to run/view it (localhost fine).
  6. README covering how to run, policy rationale, a multi-tenant scaling
     discussion (50 shops), and "what's next."
  7. Tests for the risky parts only, plus the full AI conversation history as a
     deliverable.

  A couple of judgment calls I'll need to make explicit in the README: treating
  cooldown/dedup by phone number (customer) vs. by quote_id, how to weight
  "viewed recently" vs "large amount + stale" in priority ordering, and what
  counts as a safe idempotency key for sends.

  Let me know if that matches your read of it, or if you want to discuss
  approach/stack before I start building.

❯ let me know about any ambiguities or questions i should answer before you
  start building.

⏺ Committed at /Users/elkinal/Documents/robby/followup_engine.

  Summary of what's built:
  - Ingest (ingest.py): upserts quotes by id, dedupes events by event_id, skips
    malformed lines/unknown quote_ids without aborting — all idempotent on
    re-run.
  - Policy (policy.py): 4-tier reasoned ranking (reply_pending →
    viewed_no_response → stale_big → stale_general), computed from events sorted
    by timestamp rather than file order, deliberately ignoring the seed's stale
    last_contact_at field.
  - Drafting (drafting.py): deterministic templates keyed by reason code.
  - Service/guardrails (service.py): draft → approve → send state machine;
    cooldown scoped by customer phone (not quote, catching the Q-1003/Q-1025
    duplicate-customer case); DB-level unique index backstopping "one open draft
    per quote"; idempotent send via INSERT OR IGNORE keyed on message_id;
    re-validates quote status at send time.
  - CLI (cli.py): ingest, candidates, draft, drafts, approve, send, outbox.
  - 24 passing tests covering dedup/idempotent ingest, out-of-order events, each

⏺ Fully implemented — all 5 "what to build" items from the assignment are done
  and verified working:

  1. Ingest — ingest.py loads quotes.json/events.jsonl into SQLite, dedupes by
     event_id, upserts quotes by id, skips malformed/unknown-quote events
     without crashing. Idempotent on re-run.
  2. Follow-up policy — policy.py, 4-tier reasoned ranking (reply pending →
     viewed-no-response → stale big quote → stale general), derived from events
     sorted by timestamp (not file order).
  3. Message drafting — drafting.py, deterministic templates keyed to the match
     reason.
  4. Draft → approve → send with guardrails — service.py: never touches
     accepted/dismissed quotes (checked twice — candidate generation and send
     time), cooldown scoped per customer phone number, idempotent send keyed by
     message_id, DB-level unique index as a backstop against duplicate open
     drafts.
  5. Way to run it — CLI (cli.py) with
     ingest/candidates/draft/drafts/approve/send/outbox subcommands.

  Tests: 24 passing (unittest, stdlib only) covering ingest dedup/idempotency,
  each policy rule, out-of-order event handling, cross-quote cooldown,
  double-send prevention, and the accept-between-approve-and-send race
  condition.

⏺ Here's the project walked through in the order data flows through it.

  followup_engine/timeutil.py — timestamp helpers

  - All timestamps are ISO-8601 UTC strings ending in Z (e.g.
    2026-08-15T00:00:00Z), matching the seed data.
  - parse_ts() converts that string → a Python datetime.
  - format_ts() converts a datetime back → that string format.
  - utcnow_str() gets the real current time in that format.
  - Exists so every other file parses/formats timestamps the same way, once.

  followup_engine/db.py — database schema

  - Opens a SQLite connection and creates 4 tables if they don't exist:
    - quotes — mirrors quotes.json (id, customer, amount, status, etc.)
    - events — the ingested event log, one row per event_id (deduped)
    - messages — our own draft/approved/sent messages (the thing we create)
    - outbox — the actual "sent" record; stands in for a real SMS/email provider
  - Two guardrails are enforced as database constraints, not just app logic:
    - A unique partial index says a quote can have at most one draft/approved
      message open at a time.
    - outbox.message_id is a primary key, which is what makes sending idempotent
      (explained below).

  followup_engine/ingest.py — loading the seed data in

  - ingest_quotes() reads quotes.json, upserts each quote by id (insert, or
    overwrite if it already exists).
  - ingest_events() reads events.jsonl line by line:
    - Skips blank lines, invalid JSON, and events referencing a quote_id that
      doesn't exist — logs them instead of crashing.
    - Inserts each event with INSERT OR IGNORE, keyed by event_id → duplicate
      lines become no-ops.
  - ingest_all() just runs both in order (quotes first, since events need to
    validate against them).
  - Running this command twice changes nothing the second time — that's
    intentional.

  followup_engine/policy.py — deciding who to follow up on

  - compute_signals() reads all events sorted by timestamp (not file order,
    since the stream is out-of-order) and, per quote, finds:
    - last time it was viewed
    - last time the customer replied
    - last time we sent a message
  - evaluate_quote() checks each open quote against 4 rules, in priority order
    (first match wins):
    a. reply_pending — customer replied, we haven't answered → most urgent
    b. viewed_no_response — viewed in the last 72h, no reply, no nudge yet
    c. stale_big — no contact in 4+ days, amount ≥ $5,000
    d. stale_general — no contact in 7+ days, any amount
  - get_candidates() runs this for every open quote and returns them sorted (by
    tier, then by urgency within the tier).
  - last_outbound_by_phone() — same signals, but grouped by phone number instead
    of quote, so two quotes belonging to the same customer are treated as one
    person for cooldown purposes.

  followup_engine/drafting.py — writing the message

  - A dictionary of plain string templates, one per reason_code.
  - draft_message() picks the template matching the candidate's reason and fills
    in name/tech/amount.
  - No AI/external calls — fully deterministic and testable.

  followup_engine/service.py — the draft → approve → send pipeline (guardrails
  live here)

  - create_drafts():
    - Gets candidates from policy.py.
    - Skips a candidate if their phone number was contacted within the last 48h
      (cooldown) — checked across all their quotes, not just this one.
    - Inserts a draft row; if the DB's unique-index guardrail rejects it
      (duplicate open draft), skips gracefully instead of crashing.
  - approve(): flips a message draft → approved. Refuses if it isn't currently a
    draft.
  - send():
    - Refuses if the message isn't approved (can't send an unapproved draft).
    - If it's already sent, returns immediately — no-op, not an error.
    - Re-checks the quote is still open right before sending (in case it got
      accepted in the meantime) — if not, marks the message failed and refuses.
    - Writes to outbox via INSERT OR IGNORE keyed by message_id — this is the
      idempotency trick: retrying the exact same send can never create a second
      outbox row.

  followup_engine/cli.py — the user-facing commands

  - Wires everything above into subcommands: ingest, candidates, draft, drafts,
    approve, send, outbox.
  - --now lets you simulate "today" against the Aug-2026 seed data; omit it in
    real use to use the real clock.
  - Catches GuardrailViolation/ValueError and prints a clean error instead of a
    stack trace.

  tests/ — verifying all of the above

  - helpers.py — shared test setup (in-memory DB, quick quote/event inserts).
  - test_ingest.py — dedup, idempotent re-ingest, malformed/unknown data
    handling.
  - test_policy.py — each of the 4 rules fires correctly, priority ordering,
    out-of-order events still resolve correctly.
  - test_guardrails.py — cross-quote cooldown, no duplicate drafts, send
    requires approval, idempotent send, the "accepted mid-flow" race condition.

  README.md

  - How to run it, the policy rationale (with the judgment calls explained), the
    50-shop scaling discussion, and what's next.

  Data flow in one line: quotes.json/events.jsonl → ingest.py → SQLite →
  policy.py ranks open quotes → drafting.py writes the text → service.py
  enforces guardrails through draft/approve/send → outbox table is the final
  "sent" record — all driven through cli.py.

⏺ Good questions — let me actually re-audit the code rather than just recall it.
  I found real gaps in all four areas. Let me walk through each, then fix
  what's fixable.

  1. DB constraints — gaps found

  Currently enforced at the DB level: quote/message status enums,
  one-open-draft-per-quote, outbox idempotency key. Missing:

  - events.quote_id has no foreign key to quotes — only checked in application
    code (ingest.py), so a direct write could create a dangling event.
  - Nothing stops amount from being negative or zero.
  - Nothing stops a message being sent/approved without sent_at/approved_at
    being set (a state the code never produces today, but nothing guarantees it
    won't in the future).
  - Biggest one: the "never message a non-open quote" guardrail is Python-only.
    It's enforced by filtering in policy.py and re-checked in service.send(),
    but nothing stops a bug from directly inserting a draft, approving one, or
    writing to outbox for a closed quote. That's exactly the kind of thing you
    said should be structurally impossible, not just checked.

  2. Timestamp handling — a real crash bug

      from followup_engine.timeutil import parse_ts…)

      import json, tempfile, sqlite3…)

  its value is malformed/null — which the current field-presence check doesn't
  catch.

      import json, tempfile…)

⏺ Confirmed worse than I thought: a single malformed row (key present, value
  null — which the field-presence check doesn't catch) raises an uncaught
  IntegrityError, aborts the whole ingest, loses rows that came after it in the
  file, and would print a raw Python traceback to the user since nothing in
  cli.py catches it. Let's also check the drafting bug I spotted on
  read-through.

⏺ Bash(python3 -c "
      from followup_engine.drafting import draft_message…)
  ⎿  (No output)

⏺ Confirmed — a blank/whitespace customer_name crashes draft_message, which
  would abort the entire draft CLI run (not just skip that one candidate).

  3. Test coverage — not full, and I can show where

  - No tests for cli.py, timeutil.py, or drafting.py at all.
  - No test for null-valued-but-present fields, non-dict rows, or non-numeric
    amount in ingest — the crash above wasn't caught by any test.
  - No end-to-end test asserting the final sort order across multiple tiers in
    one batch — I only tested that each rule fires with the right reason_code
    individually. (This matters: I introduced a real double-negation sorting bug
    earlier that only got caught by eyeballing CLI output, not by a test.)
  - No boundary tests at the exact threshold (e.g. stale_for exactly equal to 7
    days, cooldown exactly at 48h).

  4. Other edge cases found on re-read

  - Phone number isn't normalized. The cross-quote cooldown guardrail keys on
    customer_phone as a raw string — "+19175552003" vs "19175552003" for the
    same real person would be treated as two different customers, silently
    breaking the guardrail it exists for.
  - Channel is never customer-aware. message_sent/customer_replied events carry
    a channel (sms/email), but our own send() always defaults to "sms" — we
    could text someone who has only ever emailed.
  - Duplicate quote id within quotes.json itself is silently resolved
    last-write-wins with no warning.
  - --now isn't validated against reality — nothing stops approve/send from
    being called with a --now earlier than the message's own created_at,
    producing a nonsensical negative-duration audit trail (minor, since --now is
    a demo/testing convenience, not a production input).
  - No record of who approved — relevant for the "human in the loop" audit
    trail, not just a technical gap.
  - SQLite single-writer: concurrent CLI invocations can hit database is locked,
    unhandled. Already flagged at a high level in the README's 50-shop section,
    but worth being explicit it's a real current gap, not just a future scaling
    concern.

  ---

  Given you said to hard-constrain the DB wherever possible, let me fix the
  concrete ones now: add the missing FK, value constraints, and — the important
  one — three triggers that make "draft/approve/send for a non-open quote"
  structurally impossible at the DB layer, not just checked in Python. Then I'll
  harden ingest against bad rows, fix the drafting crash, and backfill the test
  gaps (sort-order, boundaries, the new constraints, ingest robustness,
  timeutil).

      Harden DB constraints and fix crash bugs found in guardrail audit…)
  ⎿  (No output)

⏺ Committed. Summary of this audit pass:

  DB constraints — added what was missing: events.quote_id FK, amount >= 0,
  approved_at/sent_at consistency checks, and — the important one — three
  triggers that make "draft/approve/send for a non-open quote" structurally
  impossible at the schema level, not just checked in Python. Verified by
  bypassing service.py entirely and writing raw SQL directly at the invalid
  states.

  Timestamps — found and fixed two real crash bugs: ingest didn't validate field
  values (only key presence), so a present-but-null amount or unparseable
  created_at would raise an uncaught IntegrityError mid-batch and silently lose
  every row after it (confirmed, then fixed); parse_ts(None) raised
  AttributeError instead of a catchable error, which let a literal JSON null
  timestamp slip past the first fix (also confirmed, then fixed). Offsets, naive
  timestamps, and Z-suffixed timestamps were already handled correctly.

  Test coverage — was not full; added 24 new tests (48 total) closing the gaps:
  DB constraints/triggers, ingest value-validation, timeutil edge cases,
  drafting edge cases, and — notably — an end-to-end multi-tier sort-order test
  and threshold-boundary tests that didn't exist before (the kind of test that
  would have caught the sort-key bug I introduced and fixed earlier in this
  session).

  Other edge cases — fixed the draft_message() crash on a blank customer name;
  documented (not fixed, by design) the remaining gaps in the README:
  unnormalized phone numbers breaking cross-quote cooldown, channel never being
  customer-aware, no approver identity, and SQLite's single-writer limitation.

❯ lmk what issues you found and how you resolved them. in a concise way, with
  sets of bullet points

⏺ Issues found & fixed

  - Ingest crashed on malformed values, losing all rows already processed — a
    key being present (e.g. amount) with a null/bad-typed value passed the old
    presence-only check, then hit a NOT NULL/type error uncaught, aborting the
    whole batch mid-transaction.
    → Added real value validation (type, non-empty, parseable timestamp) per row
    in ingest.py, wrapped each insert in try/except so one bad row is skipped
    and logged, not fatal.
  - parse_ts(None) raised AttributeError, not a catchable error — a literal JSON
    null timestamp slipped past the ingest fix above and still crashed.
    → timeutil.parse_ts now raises TypeError for any non-string input, so
    ingest's except (TypeError, ValueError) actually catches it.
  - draft_message() crashed on a blank/whitespace customer name — "".split()[0]
    → IndexError, would've taken down the whole draft command for that
    candidate.
    → Added a safe fallback ("there") when the name has no parseable first word.
  - The "never message a non-open quote" guardrail only lived in Python —
    nothing stopped a direct DB write from creating an invalid state.
    → Added three schema-level triggers (block draft insert / approve / outbox
    insert for a non-open quote), so it's structurally impossible rather than
    just checked. Verified by writing raw SQL directly against the DB, bypassing
    service.py entirely.
  - Weaker DB constraints generally — missing FK on events.quote_id, no floor on
    amount, no consistency check between status and approved_at/sent_at.
    → Added FK, CHECK(amount >= 0), and CHECK constraints tying status to the
    right timestamps being set.
  - Test coverage gaps — no tests for cli.py/timeutil.py/drafting.py, no test of
    the final sorted order across mixed tiers (only individual rule firing), no
    boundary tests at exact thresholds.
    → Added 24 tests (48 total): DB constraint/trigger tests, ingest
    value-validation tests, timeutil edge cases, drafting edge cases, a full
    4-tier sort-order test, and threshold-boundary tests.

  Found, documented, intentionally not fixed

  - customer_phone isn't normalized — same person with differently formatted
    numbers would bypass the cross-quote cooldown guardrail.
  - send() always defaults to SMS, ignoring which channel the customer has
    actually used.
  - No record of who approved a message (no audit trail of approver identity).
  - SQLite is single-writer — concurrent CLI calls can hit "database is locked,"
    unhandled.

  All of this is now in the README's "Gaps I found and either fixed or
  consciously left" section, and committed in 904e687.

❯ great. do another pass over just in case. look for edge cases, failure modes.
  lmk what you find

⏺ Confirmed — a genuinely valid, parseable timestamp with a non-Z offset gets
  silently dropped from signal computation because the DB stores the raw string
  and all SQL comparisons (WHERE timestamp <= ?, ORDER BY timestamp) are
  lexicographic string comparisons, which only agree with chronological order if
  every row is in the exact same canonical format. My earlier fix (accept any
  parseable ISO-8601 format) actually made this worse — it lets non-canonical
  formats into storage where they silently corrupt ordering. Fixing it by
  normalizing every timestamp to the canonical Z form at ingest time:

      Second audit pass: fix timestamp normalization bug and CLI error leaks…)
  ⎿  (No output)

⏺ Committed (b41b1d2, 59 tests now). Here's what this pass found:

  New issues found & fixed

  - Timestamp normalization bug (the meaty one) — timestamps were stored exactly
    as received, but policy.py filters/sorts them with raw SQL string
    comparison (WHERE timestamp <= ?, ORDER BY timestamp). A perfectly valid,
    parseable timestamp in a non-Z form (e.g. 2026-08-15T10:00:00+08:00) sorts
    incorrectly as a string even though it's chronologically fine — I reproduced
    a case where a real event silently vanished from signal computation because
    of this. Ironically, my earlier fix (accept any parseable ISO-8601 format)
    made this worse by letting non-canonical formats into storage.
    → Now every timestamp is normalized to canonical Z-form via
    format_ts(parse_ts(...)) before it's written, so stored timestamps are
    guaranteed sortable as strings.
  - event_id reused for different content was silently swallowed — the dedup
    logic assumed same-id-means-same-event (byte-identical redelivery), but
    nothing verified that. A genuinely different event colliding on an id (e.g.
    a customer_replied landing on an id already used by a quote_viewed)
    disappeared into the generic "duplicate" count with no trace — reproduced,
    confirmed the real event was lost.
    → Now compares content on collision; true duplicates still count as
    duplicates, a mismatch gets surfaced in events_skipped.
  - CLI leaked raw Python tracebacks on: missing quotes.json/events.jsonl, a
    --db path whose parent directory doesn't exist, and quotes.json not being a
    JSON array at the top level. All confirmed via direct repro, all now produce
    clean error: ... messages with exit code 1.
  - Zero test coverage on cli.py — added test_cli.py with clean-error-message
    tests and a full ingest→draft→approve→send→outbox run through the actual CLI
    entry point (not just the underlying modules).

  Still open (documented, not fixed — unchanged from last pass)

  Phone number normalization, channel-aware sending, approver identity, SQLite
  single-writer concurrency.

  the IDE. This may or may not be related to the current

  - potential consequences
  - how it was resolved

      195 +  `normalize_phone`) to an E.164-ish canonical form before bei
          +ng stored, so

          +r resolve to

          +nt serving

          +r`); I called                                                 
      201 +  this out explicitly rather than let a hand-rolled heuristic 
          +look more                                                     
      202 +  authoritative than it is.                                   
      203 +- **Outbound channel is now chosen per-customer, not hardcoded
          + to SMS.**

          +customer's own                                                
      205 +  event history -- their most recent reply's channel wins (the

⏺ Committed (03b2b28, 82 tests passing). Here's the detailed breakdown as
  requested.

  1. Phone number normalization

  - Problem: customer_phone was stored exactly as given. The cross-quote
    cooldown guardrail keys on this field, but it was a raw string with no
    canonical form.
  - Potential consequences: The same real customer entered as "+19175552003" in
    one system and "(917) 555-2003" in another (common with multi-source data)
    would be treated as two different people. The cooldown guardrail's entire
    purpose — "don't spam this human" — would silently fail to catch that case.
  - How it was resolved: New followup_engine/phone.py with normalize_phone() —
    strips formatting, infers US country code for bare 10/11-digit numbers,
    rejects anything too short to plausibly be a phone number. Wired into
    ingest.py so every quote's phone is normalized once, at the single point of
    entry, before anything downstream (cooldown, drafts, outbox) ever sees it.
    Documented as a simplified, US-centric heuristic, not a real i18n library —
    flagged explicitly as a scope limitation rather than presented as more
    robust than it is.

  2. Channel-aware sending

  - Problem: send() always defaulted to "sms", even though the event log already
    records which channel (sms/email) a customer has actually used.
  - Potential consequences: Texting a customer who has only ever emailed is a
    real way to never reach them — the system had the signal and ignored it.
  - How it was resolved: Added QuoteSignals.preferred_channel in policy.py —
    uses the customer's most recent reply's channel (strongest signal, they
    chose it), falling back to the channel we last messaged them on, falling
    back to SMS only with zero history. Stored on the draft at creation time
    (new messages.channel column, CHECK-constrained to known channels) and used
    by send() unless explicitly overridden. Verified against the real seed data:
    Q-1007 now drafts on email because that customer's event history shows a
    customer_replied on email — not a synthetic test case.

  3. Approver identity

  - Problem: approve() recorded that a message was approved and when, but not
    who approved it.
  - Potential consequences: No audit trail for a human-in-the-loop system is a
    real gap the moment more than one person can approve sends — "who authorized
    this text to go out" becomes unanswerable.
  - How it was resolved: New messages.approved_by column, required by
    service.approve() (raises ValueError if blank), enforced alongside
    approved_at by the same CHECK-constraint pattern used elsewhere in the
    schema. CLI defaults it to getpass.getuser() (the OS user) so a single-owner
    setup needs zero extra flags, with --by to override.

  4. SQLite concurrency

  - Problem: SQLite is single-writer; concurrent CLI invocations can contend.
  - Potential consequences: A database is locked error surfacing mid-operation,
    or a writer blocking a read command unnecessarily.
  - How it was resolved, and what I corrected mid-implementation: Added PRAGMA
    journal_mode = WAL to db.connect() — this is the real fix: under WAL, a
    writer never blocks a reader, so candidates/drafts/outbox can run while a
    draft/send is in progress (I verified this isn't just theoretical). I also
    added PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000, but checking it against a plain connection
    showed Python's sqlite3 module already defaults to a 5-second busy timeout
    — so that line doesn't actually change behavior. I kept it as explicit,
    change-controlled documentation of a value the code relies on, but said so
    plainly in the README rather than claim a fix for a problem that turned out
    not to exist. This is a mitigation for a single local file, not a substitute
    for a real multi-writer datastore at the 50-shop scale already discussed in
    the README.

  All four are covered by new/extended tests (test_phone.py, test_db.py, plus
  additions to test_policy.py, test_guardrails.py, test_db_constraints.py), and
  the README's audit section documents each decision inline.

⏺ Current state

  All 5 "what to build" requirements from the assignment are implemented,
  hardened across three audit passes, and covered by 82 passing tests (stdlib
  unittest, no dependencies):

  - Ingest (ingest.py) — dedupes events by event_id, upserts quotes, validates
    field values (not just presence), normalizes timestamps and phone numbers to
    canonical forms, never aborts on one bad row, raises a clean IngestError
    for input-shape problems (missing file, bad JSON).
  - Policy (policy.py) — 4-tier reasoned ranking, event-sourced signals (sorted
    by timestamp, not file order), per-customer channel preference derived from
    real event history.
  - Drafting (drafting.py) — deterministic templates, safe on blank names.
  - Guardrails (service.py + schema) — never-message-closed-quote,
    cross-customer cooldown, idempotent send, approve-before-send — each
    enforced in Python and as a hard DB constraint/trigger so the invalid state
    can't be created even by a bug. Every approval now records who approved it.
  - CLI (cli.py) — ingest/candidates/draft/drafts/approve/send/outbox, all
    failure paths produce clean error messages instead of raw tracebacks.

  Three audit passes on top of the initial build found and fixed real bugs
  (crash-on-malformed-data, a timestamp string-comparison correctness bug,
  silent event collisions, missing DB constraints) and implemented four
  previously-deferred items (phone normalization, channel-aware sending,
  approver identity, WAL mode for concurrent reads). README documents the policy
  rationale, every judgment call, the 50-shop scaling discussion, and an honest
  "found but not fixed" list. 4 commits on main.

  What's left

  One required deliverable, not something I can do for you: exporting this
  conversation via Claude Code's /export and including it in the submission —
  the assignment is explicit that this is part of what you submit, and it's an
  action only you can trigger.

  Everything else remaining is optional, explicitly out-of-scope-for-MVP
  material, already listed in the README's "what I'd build next" and "found but
  not fixed" sections:
  - A real SMS/email provider behind send() (currently an outbox table stand-in)
    with async delivery status and retry/backoff.
  - Letting a human edit a draft's body before approving, not just
    approve/reject verbatim.
  - A rejected terminal state for drafts a human declines (they currently sit in
    draft forever).
  - Per-shop policy config for the 50-shop scenario, plus a backtest harness
    against historical outcomes.
  - A small local web UI instead of the CLI (explicitly not evaluated per the
    assignment, so lowest priority).
  - Fuzz/property-based testing on the ingester beyond the hand-written cases.
  - A real migration story (schema currently only grows via CREATE TABLE IF NOT
    EXISTS, so an old followup.db needs deleting across schema changes — fine
    for an MVP, documented as such).

  Nothing is broken or half-finished — the remaining items are genuine "next
  iteration" scope, not loose ends in what's already built.

❯ i would like you explain the project once again. including the new
  features/fixes that we added. file by file again. explain any decision that
  weren't obvious

⏺ Here's the full file-by-file walkthrough, updated with everything added across
  the three audit passes.

  followup_engine/timeutil.py — timestamp helpers

  - parse_ts() parses any ISO-8601 string (Z suffix, explicit offset, or naive)
    into an aware UTC datetime.
  - format_ts() converts back to the canonical ...Z string form.
  - Non-obvious fix: parse_ts now explicitly rejects non-string input with
    TypeError instead of letting None.endswith() raise a raw AttributeError.
    This matters because callers catch (TypeError, ValueError) — before the fix,
    a literal JSON null timestamp slipped past that catch and crashed
    ingestion.

  followup_engine/phone.py — new file

  - normalize_phone() strips formatting and converts a phone number to an
    E.164-ish canonical string (e.g. (917) 555-2003 → +19175552003).
  - Why it exists: the cross-quote cooldown guardrail keys on customer_phone.
    Without normalization, the same person entered in two formats would be
    treated as two different customers, silently defeating the guardrail.
  - Non-obvious decision: it's a simple regex + US-centric heuristic, not a real
    phone library — documented in the README as a known simplification, not
    oversold as more robust than it is.

  followup_engine/db.py — schema

  - Four tables: quotes, events, messages (our draft pipeline), outbox (the
    actual "sent" record).
  - New columns: messages.channel (which channel this draft was written for) and
    messages.approved_by (who approved it).
  - CHECK constraints make invalid rows unrepresentable: amount >= 0, channel IN
    ('sms','email'), and — the important one — a message can't be
    approved/sent/failed without approved_at and approved_by both set, and can't
    be sent without sent_at. These aren't "should never happen" comments; the
    database physically refuses the insert.
  - Three triggers (block_draft_for_non_open_quote,
    block_approve_for_non_open_quote, block_send_for_non_open_quote): make
    "message a non-open quote" structurally impossible at the schema level, not
    just checked in Python. I added these after noticing the guardrail only
    lived in service.py — correct for every path that goes through it today, but
    not for a future bug or direct write.
  - connect(): enables foreign keys, and now also PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL +
    PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000.
    - Non-obvious: busy_timeout doesn't actually change behavior — Python's
      sqlite3 module already defaults to a 5s timeout. I kept the line anyway as
      explicit, change-controlled documentation of a value the code depends on,
      rather than silently relying on a stdlib default. WAL is the real fix: it
      lets reads (candidates/drafts/outbox) proceed without blocking on a
      concurrent write.

  followup_engine/ingest.py — loading data in

  - _load_json_list() validates the input is actually readable JSON and a
    top-level array, raising a dedicated IngestError (distinct from a single bad
    row) — missing file, invalid JSON, or wrong top-level shape no longer crash
    with a raw traceback.
  - _normalize_quote_row() validates and normalizes a quote in one pass: rejects
    non-dict rows, bad status, non-string/empty id or name, non-numeric or
    negative amount — and critically, normalizes customer_phone via phone.py and
    both timestamps via format_ts(parse_ts(...)) before they're ever stored.
    - Non-obvious and important: timestamps are normalized to the canonical Z
      form, not just validated as parseable. policy.py compares/sorts timestamps
      with raw SQL string comparison (WHERE timestamp <= ?, ORDER BY
      timestamp), which only agrees with real chronological order if every row
      uses the identical format. A validly-parseable timestamp with a +08:00
      offset, stored verbatim, would sort wrong and silently vanish from signal
      computation — I reproduced this, then fixed it by normalizing at the point
      of entry rather than trusting storage to "just work."
  - Every row-level insert is wrapped in try/except sqlite3.IntegrityError, so
    one bad row is skipped and logged, never aborts rows around it or loses an
    uncommitted batch.
  - Event collision detection: when an event_id already exists, the code now
    compares the new event's content against what's stored. A byte-identical
    redelivery still counts as a harmless duplicate; genuinely different content
    reusing the same id (which should never happen) is surfaced in
    events_skipped instead of silently vanishing into the duplicate count.

  followup_engine/policy.py — the ranking logic

  - Same 4-tier rule set as before (reply_pending → viewed_no_response →
    stale_big → stale_general), derived from events sorted by timestamp, with
    quotes.last_contact_at deliberately untrusted (it disagrees with the event
    log in the seed data).
  - New: QuoteSignals.preferred_channel — picks which channel (sms/email) to
    draft on, from the customer's own history: their most recent reply channel
    wins (they chose it), falling back to the channel we last messaged them on,
    falling back to "sms" only with zero history or an unrecognized channel
    value.
    - Non-obvious: reply channel wins even if it's older than a later outbound
      send on a different channel — the idea is "what channel does this person
      actually use," not "what did we do most recently."
  - Candidate now carries a channel field, set from sig.preferred_channel at
    every one of the four rule sites.

  followup_engine/drafting.py — message templates

  - Unchanged logic, one fix: _first_name() falls back to "there" for a
    blank/whitespace-only customer_name instead of crashing with IndexError on
    "".split()[0].

  followup_engine/service.py — guardrails and state transitions

  - create_drafts(): now writes c.channel into the new messages.channel column.
    The IntegrityError handler distinguishes "quote no longer open" (the new DB
    trigger firing, a race-condition backstop) from "already has an open draft"
    (the unique index) by inspecting the error text, so the reported skip reason
    stays accurate instead of being generically wrong.
  - approve(): now requires approved_by: str (raises ValueError if blank) and
    writes it alongside approved_at. Also wraps the UPDATE in try/except to
    catch the new block_approve_for_non_open_quote trigger and convert it into a
    clean GuardrailViolation — without this, a quote closing between draft and
    approval would leak a raw SQLite error.
  - send(): channel parameter now defaults to None, meaning "use the channel
    this draft was written for" (row["channel"]); an explicit value overrides
    it. Everything else — idempotent INSERT OR IGNORE into outbox keyed by
    message_id, re-checking the quote is still open right before sending — is
    unchanged.

  followup_engine/cli.py — commands

  - cmd_approve: resolves --by via getpass.getuser() if not given, so a
    single-owner setup needs no extra flag.
  - cmd_send: --channel is now an optional override (default=None) instead of
    always forcing "sms".
  - cmd_drafts: now prints [channel] and approved_by=... when present.
  - main(): now also catches ingest.IngestError and sqlite3.OperationalError
    alongside the existing guardrail/value errors — and for OperationalError
    specifically, distinguishes "database is busy, try again" (lock contention)
    from "could not open database" (bad path) by inspecting the error text,
    instead of giving one generic, sometimes-wrong message.

  tests/ — what protects all of this

  - helpers.py: shared in-memory DB + quote/event insert helpers for tests.
  - test_ingest.py: dedup, idempotent re-ingest, malformed/null-valued fields,
    non-dict/non-list JSON shapes, timestamp normalization, event-id
    content-collision detection, phone normalization.
  - test_policy.py: each rule individually, a full 4-tier sort-order test (added
    after I introduced and fixed a real sort-key bug earlier),
    threshold-boundary tests, and a new TestPreferredChannel class covering the
    channel-selection logic.
  - test_guardrails.py: cross-customer cooldown, idempotent send, the
    accept-mid-flow race, approver-required, approver recorded correctly, send
    defaults to/can override the draft's channel.
  - test_db_constraints.py: direct raw-SQL tests proving the three triggers and
    CHECK constraints reject invalid states even when service.py is bypassed
    entirely.
  - test_db.py (new): confirms the WAL/busy_timeout/foreign_keys pragmas
    actually took effect on a connection.
  - test_phone.py (new), test_drafting.py, test_timeutil.py, test_cli.py (new —
    the CLI layer had zero coverage before; now covers clean-error-message paths
    and a full CLI-driven ingest→draft→approve→send→outbox run).

  82 tests total, all passing, stdlib-only.

  README.md

  - Run instructions, policy rationale, the 50-shop scaling discussion, and
    three dated "gaps found and fixed/not fixed" subsections documenting every
    decision above in the candidate's own words — including the honest
    correction that busy_timeout doesn't actually change behavior, rather than
    letting that line imply a fix that isn't real.

❯ how did we decide what to put in the tests
⏺ The test suite wasn't built to a coverage number — it grew from a few
  different, deliberate selection criteria, applied at different points in the
  conversation:

  1. The assignment's own instruction set the baseline philosophy
  - It explicitly says "tests for the parts you think deserve tests... not 100%
    coverage... show what you consider risky." That framing was taken literally:
    skip trivial code (dataclasses, simple getters, argument parsing itself),
    concentrate on anything where being wrong has a real consequence.

  2. Things the assignment explicitly calls "the point" got tested first, in the
  initial build
  - Guardrails: cooldown, never-message-closed-quote, idempotent send,
    approve-before-send — these are literally named in the spec as what's being
    evaluated, so each got a direct test proving it holds, plus an adversarial
    variant (cooldown across different quotes for the same customer, a race
    where the quote closes between approve and send).
  - "Messy input" handling: the spec says events are duplicated and arrive out
    of order "intentionally" — so dedup and out-of-order resolution got tests
    from the start, not as an afterthought.
  - Each policy rule got its own test, because the rules are the core judgment
    call the README has to defend — if a rule's logic is wrong, that's the one
    thing in this project that's hardest to catch by inspection.

  3. Every bug found during the audit passes became a regression test, not just
  a fix
  - This was reactive, not planned: write a repro script to prove the bug is
    real → fix the code → convert the repro into a permanent test. Examples: the
    timestamp-normalization bug, the event_id-reused-for-different-content bug,
    the blank-name crash in drafting.py, the null-valued-field ingest crash,
    parse_ts(None) raising the wrong exception type. None of these were "let's
    test edge cases for completeness" — each one came from an actual failure I
    reproduced first.

  4. Gaps were found by asking "what module has zero tests" and checking if
  that's actually safe
  - cli.py, timeutil.py, drafting.py, phone.py, and the DB pragmas all had no
    coverage at some point. The question wasn't "should everything be tested" —
    it was "does this specific untested thing contain real logic" (yes for all
    five: CLI has error-handling branches, drafting.py has string manipulation
    that can crash, timeutil.py is load-bearing for every timestamp comparison
    in the system). Once the answer was yes, tests got added; a file that's pure
    wiring with no branches wouldn't have triggered this.

  5. One category exists specifically to catch my own mistake
  - The individual policy-rule tests all passed even when the final sort order
    was wrong (I introduced a real double-negation bug in the sort keys that no
    per-rule test caught, only manual CLI inspection did). That taught a
    specific lesson applied afterward: test the end-visible behavior (the actual
    ranked list), not just that each mechanism fires — so a full 4-tier
    ordering test and threshold-boundary tests got added as a deliberate
    category, not just more of the same.

  6. DB constraint tests exist to verify the schema independent of the app
  - Once hard constraints/triggers were added at the DB layer, those got tests
    that insert directly via raw SQL, bypassing service.py entirely — proving
    the constraint holds even if the Python guardrail were buggy or bypassed,
    which is the whole point of putting it in the schema in the first place.

  What deliberately stayed out: exhaustive input-permutation fuzzing, testing
  argparse's own behavior, concurrency/locking under real multi-process load
  (flagged in the README as a known, accepted gap rather than tested), and
  anything where a test would just be re-asserting a line of code with no
  judgment behind it.

❯ let's do a runthrough of the tests

⏺ Bash(python3 -m unittest discover -s tests -v 2>&1)
  ⎿  (No output)

⏺ All 82 tests pass. Here's the runthrough, grouped by file in the order they'd
  matter to a reviewer:

  test_policy.py (18 tests) — the ranking logic
  - TestPolicyRules (13): each of the 4 rules fires correctly in isolation,
    accepted/dismissed quotes are never candidates, a brand-new quote isn't
    flagged prematurely, events are evaluated by timestamp not file order,
    future events are ignored, plus the full 4-tier sort-order test and a
    threshold-boundary test (exactly 7 days vs. 1 second short).
  - TestPreferredChannel (5): channel defaults to SMS with no history, a reply's
    channel wins, it wins even over a later outbound send on a different
    channel, falls back to last-outbound channel with no reply, falls back to
    default on an unrecognized channel value.

  test_guardrails.py (11 tests) — the safety properties
  - TestDraftGuardrails (5): cooldown applies across a customer's different
    quotes, different customers don't cool each other down, a recent outbound
    message blocks a new draft, re-running draft generation doesn't duplicate,
    the DB constraint rejects a second open message as a backstop.
  - TestApproveSendGuardrails (6): can't send before approval, send is
    idempotent under retry (checked by counting outbox rows, not just return
    values), send refuses a quote that got accepted mid-flow, can't re-approve
    an already-sent message, approval requires a non-blank approver, approver
    identity is actually recorded, send defaults to / can override the draft's
    channel.

  test_db_constraints.py (10 tests) — proving the schema itself, independent of
  service.py
  - Raw SQL directly against the DB: can't insert a draft for an
    accepted/dismissed quote, can't approve once the quote's gone non-open, can
    still approve while it stays open (the negative-space check), can't write an
    outbox row for a closed quote, negative amount rejected, unknown status
    rejected, orphan event rejected by FK, and the
    approved_at/approved_by/sent_at consistency checks each tested in isolation.

  test_ingest.py (12 tests) — messy-input handling
  - Dedup (byte-identical vs. content-collision on the same event_id — two
    different tests, deliberately distinguished), idempotent re-ingest, quote
    status upsert, malformed JSON lines, unknown quote_id, missing file,
    non-list top-level JSON, timestamp normalization (both for events and
    quotes.created_at), phone-number normalization reconciling two formats to
    one identity, unparseable phone rejected.

  test_cli.py (5 tests) — the layer that previously had zero coverage
  - Missing file, bad --db path, non-list JSON, and approving a nonexistent
    message all produce clean messages, not tracebacks; one full ingest → draft
    → approve → send → outbox run, plus a same-message retry, driven through the
    actual CLI entry point.

  test_db.py (3 tests) — connection-level setup
  - busy_timeout, journal_mode=WAL (on a real file — :memory: can't show this),
    and foreign_keys are all actually in effect after connect().

  test_phone.py (7), test_drafting.py (4), test_timeutil.py (7) — focused unit
  tests for the three smallest, most foundational modules, each pinned to a
  specific earlier bug (blank-name crash, parse_ts(None), phone-format
  equivalence) plus normal-path coverage.

  Nothing here is testing for its own sake — every file maps to either a named
  guardrail from the assignment, a rule from the policy, or a bug that was
  actually reproduced at some point in this conversation.

⏺ This is a genuine, serious bug — confirmed exploitable, and it's one of the
  three guardrails the assignment explicitly names ("never message the same
  customer twice within a cooldown window"). Root cause: create_drafts() only
  checks cooldown against historical sends and drafts created earlier in the
  same batch — not against open drafts from a previous draft run. And send()
  never re-checks cooldown at all, only quote status. Let me fix both, mirroring
  the pattern already used for the quote-status guardrail (checked at draft
  time and re-validated at send time).

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Summary

  I re-read the assignment fresh, then verified rather than asserted. Two
  different verification methods, two different classes of finding:

  Coverage measurement (installed coverage.py in a throwaway venv, not a project
  dependency): went from 81% → 95% branch coverage. Closed real gaps — the
  CLI's candidates/drafts commands had zero test coverage despite being 2 of 7
  commands; several ingest.py validation paths (including the exact
  parse_ts(None) bug from an earlier pass) had been verified manually at some
  point in this conversation but never promoted to a permanent test;
  tech_name/id were missing the value-validation every other required field
  already had. Fixed those, plus deleted one genuinely dead function. The
  remaining 5% is deliberately left — provably-unreachable defensive backstops,
  the __main__ guard, and non-deterministic timing branches — and I showed why,
  not just asserted it.

  Actually re-running the system against the real seed data found something
  coverage couldn't: running draft twice, five minutes apart, created two
  separate open drafts for the same customer (Karen Nguyen, Q-1003/Q-1025). I
  then proved it was exploitable, not just a database oddity — approving and
  sending both produced two real outbound messages to the same customer a minute
  apart, a direct violation of one of the three guardrails the assignment names
  explicitly. Root cause: cooldown was only ever checked against historical
  sends and same-batch drafts, never against an open draft from a separate,
  earlier draft invocation — and send() never re-checked cooldown at all.

  Fixed at both layers (draft-time prevention + send-time authoritative
  re-check, mirroring the pattern already used for the quote-status guardrail),
  re-verified the exact repro no longer allows double-sending, then ran an
  exhaustive check across all 30 real quotes confirming zero customers ever
  receive more than one message.

  116 tests, all passing, 7 commits on main. This was the most valuable pass of
  the audit — not because I found more bugs by reading code harder, but because
  I actually ran the thing the way a user would, twice, and watched it fail.

❯ explain in an easier to understand and more concise way

⏺ I checked this two ways:

  1. Measured test coverage (using a coverage tool, not guessing) — went from
  81% to 95%. Found a few things along the way: two CLI commands (candidates,
  drafts) had literally never been tested, a couple of validation checks I
  thought I'd tested were only ever checked by hand, and one unused function
  that I deleted. The remaining 5% uncovered is stuff that's genuinely fine to
  skip (like the if __name__ == "__main__" line).

  2. Actually ran the program twice, like a real user would — and this is the
  one that mattered. I ran the "create drafts" command, waited 5 minutes, ran it
  again. It created a second message to a customer who'd already gotten one.
  Then I proved it could actually send both: that customer received two texts a
  minute apart. That's a direct violation of the "never message the same
  customer twice" rule the assignment specifically asks for.

  The bug: the system checked "did we already text this person?" only by looking
  at messages that were actually sent. It never checked "is there a message
  waiting to be approved that we haven't sent yet?" So two separate runs could
  each create one, and nothing stopped a human from approving and sending both.

  Fixed it in two places: don't create a second draft if one's already waiting,
  and double-check right before sending, in case that slipped through anyway.

  116 tests pass now, and I re-ran the whole thing end-to-end to confirm no
  customer can get double-messaged. All committed.

❯ what is the current state of the proejct

⏺ Bash(python3 -m unittest discover -s tests 2>&1 | tail -3 && echo "---" && git
      status --short && echo "(clean if empty)" && echo "---" && git log
      --oneline)
  ⎿  (No output)

⏺ Done and verified. All 5 required pieces are built, working, and tested
  against the real seed data:

  - Ingests the messy quote/event data (dedupes, handles out-of-order events,
    skips bad rows without crashing)
  - Ranks open quotes into a prioritized follow-up list with reasons

❯ You are a skeptical senior engineer reviewing a take-home submission. Your
  job is to find real bugs by breaking it, not by reading and approving it.

  Context: this is a follow-up engine for open quotes. It ingests a messy event
  stream (duplicates, out-of-order), decides who to follow up with, drafts a
  message, and has a draft -> approve -> send flow. The spec's hard guardrails
  are:
  1. Never message the same customer twice within a cooldown window.
  2. Never message about an accepted or dismissed quote.
  3. Re-running the engine or retrying a failed send must never double-send.

  The spec also says to design "send" as if a real SMS provider sits behind an
  interface.

  Do this:
  1. Read the spec and the code. Write down every guardrail and every claim the
  code or its comments make.
  2. For each guardrail, list every way it could be violated: different input
  orderings, repeated runs, stale data, state changing between
  draft/approve/send, concurrent processes, and the same state arriving through
  different sources (the quotes file vs the event stream).

⏺ Clean. Here's the review.

  Claims extracted from the code/comments (what I tested against)

  - service.create_drafts: "safe to call repeatedly, including across separate
    invocations" (cooldown holds across runs)
  - service.send: "idempotent... will never write a second outbox row or
    re-send"; re-validates quote status "at send time"; re-validates cooldown
    "at send time... the authoritative check"
  - db.py: one open draft/approved message per quote is enforced by a real
    UNIQUE index; three triggers make drafting/approving/sending for a non-open
    quote "impossible... independent of what any Python code path checks"
  - policy.py: signals are "computed fresh... sorted by timestamp... so
    duplicate or out-of-order webhook delivery can't skew them"
  - Module docstring (ingest.py): "safe to re-run against a growing/changing
    export"

  Bugs ranked by severity (all reproduced — none are "by inspection")

  1. CRITICAL — Cooldown guardrail is bypassable under real concurrency

  Repro: two quotes, same customer, two approved messages. Opened two real
  connections in two threads, forced both to finish send()'s cooldown check
  (policy.last_outbound_by_phone) before either was allowed to write, via a
  threading.Barrier.
  Result: both sends succeeded — 2 outbox rows for the same customer, violating
  guardrail #1 directly.
  Cause: send()'s cooldown check is read-then-decide-then-write across multiple
  statements with no transaction/lock spanning them. The earlier fix (checking
  last_outbound_by_phone at send time) only works for sequential calls — it
  reads committed state, so if two sends are genuinely concurrent, neither sees
  the other's not-yet-committed write.
  Contrast test (same attack, different guardrail): the exact same
  barrier-forced race against "one open draft per quote" held — because that one
  is backed by a real SQLite UNIQUE INDEX, not just an app-level check. That's
  the fix shape: cooldown needs the same treatment (e.g., a DB-level exclusion,
  or a single transaction that both reads and writes the phone's last-contact
  state atomically, e.g. UPDATE-with-condition or BEGIN IMMEDIATE).
  Minimal fix: wrap the cooldown check + outbox insert in one BEGIN IMMEDIATE
  transaction (forces SQLite's write lock before the read, serializing
  concurrent senders), or add a real constraint (e.g., a last_sent_at column on
  a per-phone table updated via UPDATE ... WHERE now - last_sent_at >= cooldown,
  checking the row count).

  2. HIGH — quote_accepted events are completely ignored by policy logic

  Repro: quote with status: "open" in quotes.json (not yet reconciled) but a
  quote_accepted event in the stream from 2 days ago. get_candidates() still
  returns it as a stale_big candidate. grep -n "quote_accepted"
  followup_engine/*.py → zero matches anywhere; the event is stored but never
  read.
  Cause: policy._compute_quote_signals only switches on
  quote_viewed/customer_replied/message_sent; quote_accepted (explicitly named
  in the spec's event list) falls through silently.
  Why it matters: this is exactly the "same state arriving through different
  sources" case — quotes.json is a batch/snapshot source, the event stream is
  closer to real-time. If they disagree (a realistic ingestion-lag scenario, not
  contrived), the system will draft and can send a follow-up to someone who
  already said yes.
  Minimal fix: either read quote_accepted in policy.py and treat it as an
  additional closed-signal alongside quotes.status, or explicitly document that
  quotes.status is the only authoritative source and quote_accepted is
  intentionally vestigial (currently it's neither — it's silently stored and
  silently ignored, which looks like an oversight, not a decision).

  3. MEDIUM — send() uses a stale phone number snapshot

  Repro: draft created for +19175551111, then quotes.customer_phone corrected to
  +19175559999 (plausible: data correction, or a quote reassigned to a
  different customer record) before approval/send. The actual outbox row still
  shows +19175551111.
  Cause: messages.customer_phone is copied at draft time and never re-joined
  against quotes at send time, unlike quotes.status which is re-checked.
  Minimal fix: either re-read customer_phone from quotes at send time
  (consistent with how status is re-checked), or explicitly document the
  snapshot as intentional "send exactly what was approved" semantics — right now
  it's inconsistent (status is live-checked, phone isn't) with no stated reason
  for the difference.

  4. MEDIUM — An uncaught sqlite3.IntegrityError can crash send() raw

  Repro: forced the quote to flip to accepted in the narrow window between
  send()'s own Python precheck and its outbox INSERT (same race shape as #1,
  injected via the cooldown-check call that sits between them). The
  block_send_for_non_open_quote trigger correctly fires — but send() has no
  try/except around that INSERT, so sqlite3.IntegrityError propagates uncaught.
  cli.py's main() only catches
  GuardrailViolation/ValueError/IngestError/OperationalError — not
  IntegrityError — so this would surface as a raw traceback, not a clean error.
  Secondary effect: the message is left stuck in approved status forever (the
  UPDATE ... SET status='failed' never runs, since the exception aborts before
  reaching it) — no record of why, and a naive retry hits the same crash again.
  Minimal fix: wrap the outbox INSERT in try/except sqlite3.IntegrityError, mark
  the message failed, and raise a clean GuardrailViolation.

  5. LOW — README's own run instructions break for a plausible checkout layout

  Repro: cloned the repo into a directory not named followup_engine
  (/tmp/reviewer_checkout), then followed the README verbatim: cd
  followup_engine → python3 -m followup_engine.cli ingest → ModuleNotFoundError:
  No module named 'followup_engine'. Cause: the repo root and the Python
  package inside it share the identical name (followup_engine/followup_engine/),
  so cd followup_engine only lands in the right place if the outer checkout
  directory happens to already be named followup_engine — true in my dev
  environment, not guaranteed for a zip/clone a reviewer actually receives.
  Also confirmed, not hypothetical: the literal <message_id> placeholder in step
  5 of the README causes a zsh/bash parse error if pasted verbatim — this is
  exactly what happened in your own trial run earlier in this conversation.
  Minimal fix: rename either the repo root or the inner package so they don't
  collide, and replace <message_id> with a comment plus a real example or a
  shell variable pattern (MID=...).

  Verified safe (not bugs — actually tested, not assumed)

  - One-open-draft-per-quote: holds under the identical concurrency attack as #1
    (DB unique index).
  - No-double-send-on-retry: 5 truly concurrent send() calls on the same
    message_id → exactly 1 outbox row (DB primary key).
  - Crash-mid-send: simulated a crash between the outbox INSERT and the status
    UPDATE (no commit) — a second connection sees neither write. Atomic as
    claimed.

  Spec gaps / only nominally satisfied

  - "Never message the same customer twice within a cooldown window" — satisfied
    for sequential/single-process use (which is most of real-world CLI use),
    not satisfied under concurrent invocation, despite the spec explicitly
    asking you to design send() "as if a real integration sits behind your
    interface" — a real integration implies this eventually runs somewhere more
    concurrent than one terminal.
  - "Never message about an accepted or dismissed quote" — fully satisfied
    against quotes.status, only nominally satisfied against the full event
    stream, since quote_accepted (a type the spec names explicitly) is parsed
    and stored but never consulted.
  - The spec doesn't say which of quotes.json's status vs. the event stream's
    quote_accepted should win when they disagree — this is a genuine,
    undocumented ambiguity, not just an implementation gap.

  Nothing fixed, as requested.

      371 +### An adversarial review pass found a genuine concurrency bug

      377 +script against real data, not just reasoning about the code. I
          +t found one                                                   
      378 +genuinely serious bug and three smaller real ones:            
      379 +                                                              
      380 +- **Critical: the cooldown guardrail was bypassable under true
          + concurrency.**                                               
      381 +  `send()`'s cooldown re-check reads committed state and then 
          +writes based                                                  
      382 +  on what it read, across several separate statements. Two *re
          +al* OS                                                        
      383 +  threads, each with their own connection to the same file, ca
          +lling                                                         
      384 +  `send()` for two different messages to the same customer at 
          +the same                                                      
      385 +  time, could both read "no recent contact" before either had 
          +written                                                       
      386 +  anything -- a textbook check-then-act race. Reproduced deter
          +ministically                                                  
      387 +  by forcing the interleaving with a `threading.Barrier`: both
          + sends                                                        
      388 +  succeeded, two real outbox rows for the same customer. A con
          +trasting test                                                 
      389 +  against the *other* guardrail (one open draft per quote) -- 
          +the identical                                                 
      390 +  attack shape -- held, because that one is backed by a real S
          +QLite                                                         
      391 +  `UNIQUE INDEX`, not just an app-level check. That contrast i
          +s the fix:                                                    
      392 +  `send()` now wraps its guardrail re-checks and the outbox wr
          +ite in one                                                    
      393 +  `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction, so a second concurrent sender
          + blocks (via                                                  
      394 +  `busy_timeout`) until the first fully commits, then correctl
          +y sees it.                                                    
      395 +  Verified with 10 trials of genuine concurrent threads (no ar
          +tificial                                                      
      396 +  synchronization) -- 1 outbox row every time, down from 2 bef
          +ore the fix.                                                  
      397 +- **`quote_accepted` events were parsed, stored, and completel
          +y ignored.**

          +_accepted`

          +an arrive

      402 +  `policy.quote_accepted_via_events()`, checked both when gene
          +rating                                                        
      403 +  candidates and, independently, inside `send()`'s own re-chec
          +k -- the                                                      
      404 +  event can arrive after approval too, not just before draftin
          +g.                                                            
      405 +- **`send()` used a phone number snapshotted at draft time**,

      407 +  message went to the stale number -- inconsistent with quote 
          +status                                                        
      408 +  already being re-checked live at send time. Now reads the ph
          +one live too.                                                 
      409 +- **A DB trigger firing at send time could crash raw.** The   
      410 +  `block_send_for_non_open_quote` trigger is a backstop for ex
          +actly the                                                     
      411 +  race above; when it fired, the resulting `sqlite3.IntegrityE
          +rror` wasn't

⏺ All confirmed clean: 123 tests passing, working tree clean, real-seed run
  behaves correctly. Here's the full walkthrough.

  How it works, file by file

  timeutil.py — parses/formats all timestamps to one canonical UTC Z form.
  - Decision: every stored timestamp gets normalized at ingest, not just
    validated as parseable — because all comparisons downstream are raw SQL
    string comparisons, and two valid-but-differently-formatted timestamps don't
    sort correctly against each other as strings.

  phone.py — normalizes phone numbers to an E.164-ish form (+19175552003).
  - Decision: without this, the same customer entered as "+19175552003" vs
    "(917) 555-2003" would be treated as two different people, breaking the
    cooldown guardrail. Deliberately simple/US-centric, documented as a
    simplification.

  db.py — SQLite schema: quotes, events, messages (our draft pipeline), outbox
  (the "sent" record).
  - Decision: guardrails are enforced as real database constraints, not just
    Python checks — a UNIQUE INDEX caps one open draft per quote, CHECK
    constraints make a half-populated audit trail impossible, and three triggers
    make it physically impossible to draft/approve/send against a non-open
    quote, even if a future bug bypasses the Python layer.
  - Decision: WAL mode, so reads don't block behind a write.

  ingest.py — loads quotes.json/events.jsonl into SQLite.
  - Decision: validates every field's value, not just its presence (a null
    amount, a blank name, a garbage timestamp are all rejected, not just missing
    keys) — one bad row is skipped and logged, never aborts the batch.
  - Decision: a duplicate event_id with identical content is a harmless
    re-delivery; the same id reused for different content is flagged, not
    silently merged into the dup count. Same treatment for a duplicate quote id
    within one file.

  policy.py — given "now", ranks open quotes into a prioritized follow-up list
  with a reason.
  - Decision: signals (viewed/replied/messaged) are derived from the event log,
    sorted by timestamp — not file order, not quotes.json's own last_contact_at
    field (which is demonstrably stale in the seed data).
  - Decision: a quote_viewed is interest, not contact — it doesn't reset the
    "needs follow-up" clock.
  - Decision: 4 tiers, highest to lowest urgency: customer replied and we
    haven't answered → viewed recently, no reply → big quote gone stale → any
    quote gone stale.
  - Decision: cooldown and outbound-channel preference are tracked per customer
    phone, not per quote — one customer can have two open quotes, and texting
    her about one should count against texting her about the other.
  - Decision: a quote_accepted event is honored independently of quotes.json's
    status field, since that field can be a stale batch snapshot lagging a more
    real-time event stream.

  drafting.py — template-based message text, keyed by the reason. No AI/external
  calls — deterministic and testable.

  service.py — the only module that writes messages/outbox; every guardrail
  lives here.
  - Decision: create_drafts() won't draft a second message to a customer who
    already has an open, unsent draft — even from a separate, earlier run.
  - Decision: send() re-validates everything live, immediately before sending —
    quote status, the quote_accepted event, cooldown, and the current phone
    number — none of it trusts what was true when the draft was created.
  - Decision: send()'s guardrail checks and its write run inside one BEGIN
    IMMEDIATE transaction, so two genuinely concurrent sends can't both read
    "all clear" before either writes.
  - Decision: the actual send is INSERT OR IGNORE keyed by message_id — retrying
    a send, or re-running the whole engine, can never produce a second real
    outbound message.

  cli.py — ingest / candidates / draft / drafts / approve / send / outbox. Every
  expected failure (bad file, bad id, wrong state, busy database) prints a
  clean message, never a raw traceback.

  tests/ — 123 tests. Not blanket coverage — targeted at the guardrails, the
  policy rules, messy-input handling, and every bug actually found during review
  (including two tests that force genuine multi-thread races to prove the
  concurrency fix holds, not just reasoning about it).

  README.md — how to run it, the policy's rationale, the 50-shop scaling
  discussion, what's next, and a full honest log of what was found and fixed
  across every review pass, including the time-budget disclosure.

  Is there anything left?

  I re-ran everything just now, not from memory: tests pass, git tree is clean,
  a real end-to-end run against the real seed data behaves correctly.

  Nothing left in the code or docs. The only remaining item is still the same
